Run on QDR-168 with `--safety-min-ray-stop 0.20 --safety-slow-ray 0.60`. | Passed as diagnosis. Matched-profile seed `88`: crashes `6.49%`, `99.27%` immediate, episode-step median `1`, start min ray `0.0024m`, start coverage near zero, action-speed mean `0.0008`. Held-out seeds `90/91`: crashes `7.36%`/`7.14%`, immediate fraction `99.69%`/`100%`, start min ray `0.0015m`/`0.0m`, action speed effectively zero. The likely mechanism is `spawn_radius_min=0.0`: tiny maze spawn bounds around the origin can make all 64 attempts invalid because of the center wall, then fallback also lands invalid. | Remaining QDR-172 failures are reset/start-generation failures, not policy navigation failures. QDR-175 should test a minimum spawn radius before hard-state replay or policy changes. |
| QDR-173 | `indoor_explore_spawn_margin_alignment` | Are QDR-172's remaining zero-clearance crashes caused by exploration spawns that are physically valid at `QR_DRONE_RADIUS=0.12m` but invalid for the exploration graph/cell margin `QR_EXPLORE_CELL_MARGIN=0.30m`? Hypothesis: aligning exploration spawn sampling with the exploration valid-cell margin should remove many immediate no-target/zero-clearance failures without changing policy behavior. Temporary change tested: use `QR_EXPLORE_CELL_MARGIN` for `QR_TASK_INDOOR_EXPLORE` spawn validity. | Failed and reverted. With the QDR-172 safety-gated QDR-168 policy, matched-profile seeds `88/90/91` dropped to `83.35%`/`81.99%`/`82.01%` success and obstacle contacts rose to `16.65%`/`18.01%`/`17.99%`; no timeouts. | The remaining QDR-172 failures are not solved by simply tightening spawn validity. The margin change shifts the start distribution into states the current policy handles worse. Do not keep this as the default env change without retraining/curriculum; it was reverted. |
| QDR-172 | `indoor_explore_target_ray_safety_gate` | Can a deployable local ray safety slowdown/stop close QDR-168's obstacle-contact gap without retraining? Hypothesis: QDR-169 crash states have very low min-ray clearance, so a lidar/ray-based low-level safety gate may prevent corner clips even when the high-level target is imperfect. Added optional `--safety-min-ray-stop`, `--safety-action-ray-stop`, and `--safety-slow-ray` to target-follow actions. | Passed as a strong diagnostic/scaffold. Seed-88 sweep: min-stop `0.15` gave `88.44%`, min-stop `0.20` gave `88.27%`, min-stop `0.15`/slow `0.45` gave `90.72%`, and min-stop `0.20`/slow `0.60` gave `93.51%`, all with no timeouts. Best setting held out: matched-profile seeds `88/90/91` `93.51%`/`92.64%`/`92.86%`, no timeouts, obstacle contacts `6.49%`/`7.36%`/`7.14%`, coverage about `0.80`. Stricter single-buffer profile: `92.39%`/`91.84%`/`92.08%`, no timeouts. Diagnostic on seed `88`: remaining crashes have coverage `0.004`, min ray `0.001m`, `pred_exists=0.007`, action speed `0`; these are immediate no-target/zero-clearance starts, not the policy flying into walls. | Local ray slowdown is a deployable safety scaffold and exposes the next environment issue. It is not enough to call the full problem solved, but it closes most mid-episode obstacle contacts. Next fix spawn validity for exploration rather than tuning the policy. |
| QDR-171 | `indoor_explore_target_exists_threshold_ablation` | Is QDR-168's target-existence output already separable enough that a stricter deploy-time action gate reduces false-positive crashes without retraining? Hypothesis: QDR-169 showed QDR-168 success states have high predicted existence while crash states are around `0.5`, so raising `--exists-threshold` may reduce obstacle contacts faster than changing the trainer. Eval: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-target-distill-eval experiments/qdr168/qdr168_frontier_target_slowrollout_s88.pt --seed <seed> --episodes 4096 --total-agents 2048 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --speed-fraction 0.35 --exists-threshold <threshold>`. | Failed. Seed-88 thresholds: `0.30` gave `87.12%` success / `12.88%` obstacle, slightly worse than QDR-168's `87.35%`; `0.50` gave `85.14%` / `14.86%`; `0.70` gave `47.53%` success / `12.00%` obstacle / `40.46%` timeout; `0.90` collapsed to `4.66%` success / `88.64%` timeout. Least-destructive `0.30` on seeds `90/91`: `85.91%`/`86.29%`, not better than QDR-168 `85.91%`/`86.76%`. | Target-existence output has some signal, but threshold calibration alone does not improve the policy. Raising the threshold mostly trades movement for timeouts. |
| QDR-170 | `indoor_explore_target_distill_stop_supervision_maze` | Does directly penalizing false-positive target existence fix the QDR-169 crash mode? Hypothesis: current target distillation does not constrain direction when `oracle_exists=0` and only weakly penalizes false-positive existence. Add stronger negative-existence loss, false-positive margin loss, and absent-target direction-to-zero loss. Train: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-target-distill --output experiments/qdr170/qdr170_stop_supervision_s88.pt --speed-fraction 0.35 --seq-updates 1200 --teacher-rollout-updates 120 --max-student-rollout-prob 0.8 --total-agents 2048 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --exists-loss-weight 2.0 --negative-exists-weight 4.0 --false-positive-weight 8.0 --false-positive-margin 0.10 --absent-direction-weight 1.0 --eval-after`. | Failed. Matched eval seeds `88/90/91`: `59.72%`/`59.84%`/`59.74%` success, `25.30%`/`25.07%`/`24.52%` obstacle contacts, `14.98%`/`15.09%`/`15.74%` timeouts, coverage around `0.69-0.70`. Seed-88 diagnostic: success states have high action/target mismatch (`90.8deg` pred-oracle angle), crash states still have `pred_exists=0.697` while `oracle_exists=0`, and timeout states have `pred_exists=0`. | Loss-weighted stop supervision is too blunt. It does not solve false positives; it distorts target direction, keeps many false-positive crashes, and adds stop-induced timeouts. Do not repeat this exact weighted-loss recipe. Next test target-existence calibration or collect hard-state negative replay instead. |
| QDR-169 | `indoor_explore_target_distill_failure_diagnostic` | Why does QDR-168 still hit maze obstacles? Hypotheses: (1) the learned high-level target points into lower-clearance directions than the privileged reachable-frontier target on terminal hard states; (2) target prediction is mostly good, but the fixed target-following controller cuts corners; (3) both student and oracle fail in the same low-clearance states, meaning the frontier target abstraction itself is insufficient. Added `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-target-diagnose`. Main diagnostic command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-target-diagnose experiments/qdr168/qdr168_frontier_target_slowrollout_s88.pt --seed <seed> --episodes 4096 --total-agents 4096 --num-threads 16 --speed-fraction 0.35 --controllers both`. | Passed as diagnosis. On the stricter single-buffer eval profile, student success seeds `88/90/91`: `83.36%`/`83.43%`/`83.64%`; oracle target controller: `90.97%`/`90.12%`/`90.35%`. Student crash states are not near-success: coverage mean `0.27/0.26/0.26`, min-ray mean about `0.09m`, no timeouts. In student crash states, oracle target `exists=0.0` on all three seeds, but student target `exists` is still about `0.55/0.51/0.52`, action speed about `0.18-0.19`, predicted target-ray clearance about `0.94-0.99m` versus oracle target-ray clearance about `1.54-1.60m`, and `31-34%` of crash states have predicted target clearance worse than oracle by at least `0.20m`. Matched `2048 agents / 2 buffers` eval confirms QDR-168 modestly beats QDR-167: QDR-168 `87.35%`/`85.91%`/`86.76%` versus QDR-167 `85.65%`/`85.00%`/`85.20%`. | The next lever is not more PPO reward tuning or speed sweeping. The failure is a false-positive/high-level-target safety problem: when the privileged path target disappears or says stop, the learned target module often keeps moving and clips obstacles. QDR-170 should train an explicit target-existence/stop/safety head, add hard-state supervised negatives, or use resettable hard-state recovery on target predictions. |
| QDR-168 | `indoor_explore_frontier_target_distill_slowrollout_maze` | Does training the high-level target predictor under the same safer controller speed used at eval reduce QDR-167 obstacle contacts? Hypothesis: QDR-167 trained with `speed_fraction=0.55` but evaluated best at `0.35`; matching the induced training rollout to the safer eval controller should close part of the gap to the oracle controller. Train: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-target-distill --output experiments/qdr168/qdr168_frontier_target_slowrollout_s88.pt --speed-fraction 0.35 --seq-updates 1200 --teacher-rollout-updates 120 --max-student-rollout-prob 0.8 --total-agents 2048 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --eval-after`. Eval: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-target-distill-eval experiments/qdr168/qdr168_frontier_target_slowrollout_s88.pt --speed-fraction 0.35 --episodes 4096 --total-agents 2048 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16`. | Passed as a modest improvement. Training finished with loss `0.1978`. Matched eval seeds `88/90/91`: `87.35%`/`85.91%`/`86.76%` success, `12.65%`/`14.09%`/`13.24%` obstacle contacts, no wall/ground/timeouts. The stricter single-buffer profile is lower but stable: `83.36%`/`83.43%`/`83.64%` success. | Speed-matched rollout is real but not transformative: it improves QDR-167 by about `0.9-1.7` points on the matched profile and does not close the full oracle gap. Keep it as the current target-distill checkpoint, but do not keep tuning controller speed. |
| QDR-167 | `indoor_explore_frontier_target_distill_maze` | Can a recurrent high-level module learn the reachable frontier-path target from deployable observations/history, then drive the drone with a fixed velocity controller? Hypothesis: QDR-165 proves the path target is useful when available, while QDR-166 shows PPO cannot discover it from scalar reward. Train a MinGRU target predictor from `explore_memory_obs=3` labels while zeroing those target slots in the model input; privileged target labels are training-only. Train: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-target-distill --output experiments/qdr167/qdr167_frontier_target_mingru_s88.pt --seq-updates 1200 --total-agents 2048 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --eval-after`. Eval: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-target-distill-eval experiments/qdr167/qdr167_frontier_target_mingru_s88.pt --episodes 4096 --total-agents 2048 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --speed-fraction 0.35`. | Passed as a direction proof, not solved. Matched eval seeds `88/90/91`: `85.65%`/`85.00%`/`85.20%` success, `78.58%`/`78.04%`/`78.19%` coverage, and `14.35%`/`15.00%`/`14.80%` obstacle contacts. Single-buffer eval seeds `88/90/91`: `83.58%`/`83.16%`/`83.46%` success. The single-buffer oracle target controller at the same speed reached about `90-91%` success. | This broke the half-coverage PPO plateau by a large margin without privileged actor target slots. The problem is no longer "exploration impossible" or "PPO needs another scalar reward"; a learned high-level target module works. The remaining gap is the student target/safety gap to the privileged target/controller, not basic flight. |
| QDR-166 | `indoor_explore_frontier_path_progress_maze` | Does aligning the progress reward with the reachable frontier-path observation fix QDR-165? Train: `PATH=.venv/bin:$PATH CUDA_HOME=/usr/local/cuda-13.2 NVCC_ARCH=sm_120 CC=gcc CXX=g++ PYTHON=.venv/bin/python QUAD_TIMESTEPS=25000000 scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-frontier-path-progress-train 88 25000000`. Eval: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-eval-checkpoints experiments/qdr166/quadrotor/1779273691221 --episodes 4096 --network MinGRU --use-bias 1 --obstacle-mode maze --explore-memory-obs 3 --explore-frontier-progress-scale 1.0`. | Failed. Best deterministic checkpoint `0000000022544384.bin`: `0.364%` success, `49.682%` coverage, `6.654%` obstacle contacts, `92.982%` timeout. | Path-consistent reward is not enough. The hand controller can use the path target somewhat, but PPO still converges to half-coverage timeout. Do not keep tuning scalar frontier progress; the next step is a higher-level exploration module or resettable objective. |
| QDR-165 | `indoor_explore_frontier_path_obs_maze` | Does replacing direct nearest-frontier with a reachable explored-grid frontier path target fix the wall-facing target bug? Probe: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-frontier-obs-probe --explore-memory-obs 3 --controller direct --mode maze --episodes 2048`. Train: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-frontier-path-train 88 25000000`. Eval: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-eval-checkpoints experiments/qdr165/quadrotor/1779273384963 --episodes 4096 --network MinGRU --use-bias 1 --obstacle-mode maze --explore-memory-obs 3 --explore-frontier-progress-scale 1.0`. | Failed as PPO, passed as target diagnosis. Hand controller jumped to `46.60%` success / `8.72%` obstacle / `44.67%` timeout with `60.78%` coverage, proving the path target is much better than QDR-164's direct target. PPO still failed: best deterministic checkpoint `0000000010747904.bin`, `0.292%` success, `49.779%` coverage, `6.221%` obstacle, `93.487%` timeout. | The target semantics matter, but PPO is not learning even a mediocre target-following explorer from scalar coverage/progress. Also QDR-165 had a reward/obs mismatch, fixed separately in QDR-166. |
| QDR-164 | `indoor_explore_frontier_target_obs_controller_probe` | Does the QDR-163 direct nearest-frontier observation encode a usable control target? Commands: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-frontier-obs-probe --controller direct --mode maze --episodes 2048`, `--controller avoid`, and conservative speed variants. | Failed. Direct controller: `0.50%` success, `99.50%` obstacle, `46.02%` coverage. Ray-avoid controller: `2.74%` success, `97.26%` obstacle, `49.92%` coverage. Conservative variants were worse (`0.06-0.28%` success, `99.7%+` obstacle). | Root cause found for QDR-163: nearest-frontier direction is not a valid navigation target in a maze. It often points at a frontier boundary through or into a wall. Do not train more on `explore_memory_obs=2` as-is. |
| QDR-163 | `indoor_explore_frontier_target_obs_maze` | Can PPO solve maze exploration when the actor receives direct nearest-frontier target observation (`explore_memory_obs=2`) plus frontier-progress reward? Train: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-frontier-target-train 88 25000000`. Eval: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-eval-checkpoints experiments/qdr163/quadrotor/1779272495138 --episodes 4096 --network MinGRU --use-bias 1 --obstacle-mode maze --explore-memory-obs 2 --explore-frontier-progress-scale 1.0`. | Failed. Best deterministic checkpoint `0000000004194304.bin`: `0.386%` success, `48.385%` coverage, `10.135%` obstacle, `89.479%` timeout. Later checkpoints became safer but stayed around `49-50%` coverage and near-zero success. | Explicit nearest-frontier observation does not solve the half-maze barrier. QDR-164 shows why: the target itself is bad, not just undertrained. |
| QDR-162 | `indoor_explore_frontier_progress_reward_maze` | Can a deployable frontier-progress reward break QDR-161's half-coverage stall with `explore_memory_obs=1`? Train: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-frontier-progress-train 88 25000000`. Eval: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-eval-checkpoints experiments/qdr162/quadrotor/1779272127769 --episodes 4096 --network MinGRU --use-bias 1 --obstacle-mode maze --explore-memory-obs 1 --explore-frontier-progress-scale 1.0`. | Failed. Best deterministic checkpoint `0000000002883584.bin`: `0.215%` success, `48.466%` coverage, `14.160%` obstacle, `85.626%` timeout. Later checkpoints stayed around `48-49%` coverage and mostly timed out. | Reward-only frontier progress worsened QDR-161. It encourages early movement/contact but does not teach usable frontier-seeking. |
| QDR-161 | `indoor_explore_memory_obs_maze_from_zero` | Can from-zero PPO solve the standalone maze when the actor gets a deployable onboard-map frontier summary in the otherwise-unused exploration target slots? Hypothesis: QDR-158 failed because local rays plus recurrent memory do not reliably discover frontier behavior from sparse coverage reward; explicit egocentric frontier-memory features derived from the drone's own explored grid should improve maze success without privileged waypoints or hidden targets. Code: added `explore_memory_obs`, reusing base observation slots `14..25` for 8 egocentric frontier sector scores, best frontier direction, coverage, and frontier count; no observation-size increase. Train command: `PATH=.venv/bin:$PATH CUDA_HOME=/usr/local/cuda-13.2 NVCC_ARCH=sm_120 CC=gcc CXX=g++ PYTHON=.venv/bin/python QUAD_TIMESTEPS=25000000 scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-memory-train 88 25000000`. Eval command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-eval-checkpoints experiments/qdr161/quadrotor/1779271360375 --episodes 4096 --network MinGRU --use-bias 1 --obstacle-mode maze --explore-memory-obs 1 --last 12`. | Failed. Throughput stayed useful at about `0.86M SPS`. Best 1024-step deterministic checkpoint was `experiments/qdr161/quadrotor/1779271360375/0000000017301504.bin`: `1.309%` success, `50.344%` coverage, `6.762%` obstacle contacts, `91.929%` timeout. Longer eval did not unlock it: at `2048` steps, success was `2.609%`, coverage `51.222%`, timeout `90.022%`; at `4096`, success was `3.333%`, coverage `52.026%`, timeout `84.872%`, with more wall/ground contacts. | The hand-built onboard frontier summary did not make PPO learn frontier-seeking from zero. It made the policy safer than QDR-158 but still stalled at the same half-maze coverage barrier. Do not spend more budget on this exact reward/feature pair. The next experiment needs a stronger training signal: frontier progress reward, auxiliary frontier/coverage prediction, planner-supervised waypoint/frontier target, or asymmetric critic over map/frontier state. |
| QDR-160 | `indoor_explore_frontier_teacher_distill_maze` | Can the QDR-159 privileged frontier teacher be transferred into a deployable recurrent actor on the standalone maze? Hypothesis: if native exploration observations plus MinGRU memory are sufficient, sequence behavior cloning/DAgger from a frontier teacher should beat QDR-158's `2.052%` maze success and `50.507%` coverage without privileged actor inputs. Added `scripts/quadrotor4_frontier_distill.py` and `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-frontier-distill`. | Failed as a solution, useful as diagnosis. Fair `action_repeat=1` frontier teacher on `wonko`: `91.36%` success, `8.64%` obstacle contacts. First mixed rollout distill `experiments/qdr160/qdr160_frontier_distill_maze_s88.pt` reached `49.76%` student success but `50.24%` obstacle contacts. Pure teacher-rollout BC `experiments/qdr160/qdr160_frontier_bc400_maze_s88.pt` fit low MSE (`0.0020`) but only reached `36.17%` success / `63.81%` crashes at the native velocity envelope. Slower eval improved it to about `62.28%` success but still had `37.72%` crashes. Longer DAgger to `70%` student rollout got worse: `10.84%` success, `58.61%` obstacle contacts, `30.54%` timeouts. Conservative teacher settings timed out (`0%` success, `92.75%` timeout), and no-action-history ablation is invalid under the current 60-float native ABI (`config gives 56, build expects 60`). | Privileged frontier actions contain signal, but action-level BC/DAgger is too brittle. Low MSE does not imply safe exploration; small action errors cut corners into maze walls, and more student-rollout mixing destabilizes. Do not repeat one-step frontier action cloning as the main path. If using the teacher again, distill higher-level frontier/waypoint targets, use a planner-supervised auxiliary loss, or train with resettable hard-state recovery rather than raw action MSE. |
| QDR-159 | `indoor_explore_maze_frontier_diagnostic` | Is the QDR-157/QDR-158 maze failure caused by impossible coverage geometry, disconnected spawn components, or a learnable-but-unrewarded frontier behavior? Hypothesis: a pure geometry/frontier oracle over deployable 24-ray observations should reveal whether `85%` maze coverage is physically reachable and how many viewpoint moves are needed. Commands: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-maze-probe --mode maze --episodes 256 --greedy-steps 24` and `KMP_DUPLICATE_LIB_OK=TRUE PYTHONPATH=. scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-frontier-teacher --mode maze --episodes 64 --max-steps 1024 --drone-radius 0.30 --max-vel 0.5 --kp-xy 1.0 --waypoint-radius 0.16` locally, or the same teacher command without `KMP_DUPLICATE_LIB_OK` on `wonko`. | Passed as diagnosis. Added `scripts/quadrotor4_maze_frontier_probe.py` and `scripts/quadrotor4_frontier_teacher_probe.py`. Static maze geometry has `296` valid coverage cells, `439` drone-valid cells, one connected component, and all valid cells are visible from reachable poses (`all_pose_union_coverage=1.0`). Greedy frontier oracle reaches `100%` coverage from `256/256` sampled starts within `24` viewpoint choices, with mean path length `10.18m`. Other fixed layouts are also physically coverable: center block `99.87%` mean greedy coverage and `100%` success at the `85%` gate; doorway and corridor reach `100%`. The actual native env frontier-teacher probe, using wall-derived odometry plus privileged map/frontier planning and velocity actions, solved maze `63/64` episodes (`98.44%`) in mean `23.1` control steps on both local and `wonko`; the one failure was an immediate crash from an unlucky start/control transient. | Maze is physically connected, coverable, and executable with the current low-level velocity scaffold. The learned PPO explorer fails because it does not learn frontier-seeking/mapping behavior from local new-cell reward alone. Next method should not be more blind PPO on the same reward; build a deployable map/frontier memory observation, frontier-teacher BC/DAgger, or privileged frontier-progress reward/critic. |
| QDR-158 | `indoor_explore_v0_maze_from_zero` | Can the corrected sensor-visible exploration benchmark learn the standalone maze layout from zero, or is maze failure in QDR-157 caused by a deeper reward/perception/local-decision blocker? Hypothesis: if the standalone maze is learnable with the current deployable actor and reward, then QDR-157 mostly needs a better curriculum or layout sampling; if not, the maze geometry exposes the next environment/observation weakness. Command: `PATH=.venv/bin:$PATH CUDA_HOME=/usr/local/cuda-13.2 NVCC_ARCH=sm_120 CC=gcc CXX=g++ PYTHON=.venv/bin/python QUAD_TIMESTEPS=25000000 scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-obstacle-train qdr158 4 88 25000000`. | Failed. Throughput was about `1.4-1.5M SPS`. Best checkpoint sweep result on `maze` was `experiments/qdr158/quadrotor/1779267940546/0000000019922944.bin`: `2.052%` success, `50.507%` coverage, `9.367%` obstacle contacts, `88.580%` timeout at `1024` steps. Longer `2048`-step eval only reached `2.575%` success, `51.075%` coverage, `9.844%` obstacle, `87.581%` timeout. Best checkpoint was fetched locally to `experiments/qdr158/quadrotor/1779267940546/0000000019922944.bin`. | Standalone maze is not solved by simply training on it. The policy learns partial safe coverage, then stalls around half the valid cells. Do not solve this with more random-block/mixed PPO budget first; the next step is a maze-specific diagnostic of visibility/reachable coverage, frontier signal, spawn/goal threshold, and whether the map geometry creates hidden cells that require memory or an explicit frontier objective. |
| QDR-157 | `indoor_explore_v0_layout_mixed_from_zero` | Can the corrected sensor-visible exploration benchmark train directly on the occluded fixed-layout mixture, and does that improve the QDR-156 generalization failure without sacrificing random-block/open-room behavior? Hypothesis: if QDR-156's crashy `layout_mixed` stress is mostly train-distribution mismatch, the same deployable actor/reward/control setup should learn safer exploration when trained on `obstacle_mode=layout_mixed`. Command: `PATH=.venv/bin:$PATH CUDA_HOME=/usr/local/cuda-13.2 NVCC_ARCH=sm_120 CC=gcc CXX=g++ PYTHON=.venv/bin/python QUAD_TIMESTEPS=25000000 scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-layout-train 88 25000000`. | Useful partial pass, not promoted as solved. Added `scripts/quadrotor4_explore_gauntlet.py`, `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-gauntlet`, and `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-obstacle-train`. Local checks passed: `bash -n scripts/quadrotor4.sh`, Python compile, and `scripts/quadrotor4.sh build-cpu`; local native-bin gauntlet is blocked by the Mac CPU backend lacking CUDA `create_pufferl`, so gauntlet eval runs on `wonko`. QDR-156 full gauntlet baseline: mean success `62.482%`, mean coverage `72.134%`, mean crash `26.088%`; hardest cases were center block `61.754%` success / `38.246%` crash, corridor `64.041%` / `35.959%` crash, and maze `0.515%` / `63.845%` crash / `35.639%` timeout. QDR-157 best layout-mixed checkpoint sweep: `experiments/qdr157/quadrotor/1779267643027/0000000022544384.bin`, `62.487%` layout-mixed success, `76.055%` coverage, `7.290%` obstacle contacts, `30.223%` timeout at `1024` steps. Full 1024-step gauntlet for that checkpoint: mean success `68.382%`, mean coverage `74.812%`, mean crash `7.702%`, but mean timeout `23.916%`; corridor became `99.989%`, random blocks stayed `99.116%`, open stayed `100%`, center block stayed weak at `62.166%` success / `37.834%` crash, and maze was `0%` success with `92.142%` timeout. At `2048` steps, layout-mixed rose to `89.613%` success with `10.387%` obstacle contacts and no timeouts, while standalone maze remained `0%` success and about `49.1%` coverage. Best checkpoint was fetched locally to `experiments/qdr157/quadrotor/1779267643027/0000000022544384.bin`. | Training on the fixed-layout mixture fixes a real distribution mismatch and makes the explorer safer, but it mostly trades crashes for timeouts. The new blocker is specific: the standalone maze is not being explored past about half coverage, and center block still has a contact mode. Treat QDR-157 as a better diagnostic checkpoint, not a promoted explorer. |
| QDR-156 | `indoor_explore_v0_from_zero_random_blocks` | Can native PPO train a deployable actor from zero on the corrected sensor-visible exploration benchmark? Hypothesis: with reward tied to onboard-ray-visible coverage, random-block layouts, no waypoint/target breadcrumb, no timeout penalty, MinGRU memory, previous action, and velocity action scaffold, PPO should learn to move deliberately through occluded layouts and improve coverage/new-cell rate without simply increasing crashes. | Passed on the training distribution; failed first generalization stress. Added commands: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-train 88 25000000`, `scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-eval-checkpoints latest --episodes 4096 --last 8 --network MinGRU --use-bias 1`, and eval profiles that report `coverage`, `explored_cells`, and `new_cell_rate`. Local checks passed: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh build-cpu`, `bash -n scripts/quadrotor4.sh`, Python compile for eval scripts, and direct CPU env smoke. `beeblebrox`/several Liftarit hosts had NVML mismatch, but `wonko` was healthy (`RTX 5070`, driver `580.159.04`), so the run used `wonko` through the existing `kosh.aalto.fi` jump path. Training command on remote: `PATH=.venv/bin:$PATH CUDA_HOME=/usr/local/cuda-13.2 NVCC_ARCH=sm_120 CC=gcc CXX=g++ PYTHON=.venv/bin/python QUAD_TIMESTEPS=25000000 scripts/quadrotor4.sh explore-v0-train 88 25000000`. Throughput was about `0.78M SPS`; 25M steps finished in about `31s`. Best sampled native checkpoint sweep: `experiments/qdr156/quadrotor/1779265040848/0000000024903680.bin` with `success=99.754%`, `coverage=85.226%`, `new_cell_rate=4.81`, `crashed=0.246%`, `obstacle_hit=0.225%`, `wall_hit=0.020%`, `timeout=0%`. Open-room eval: `100%` success, `85.336%` coverage, no crashes. Layout-mixed stress eval: `55.718%` success, `70.552%` coverage, `44.282%` crashes (`42.648%` obstacle, `1.634%` wall), no timeouts. Deterministic PyTorch eval is currently blocked on the remote because the native backend was built bf16 and `torch_pufferl` requires `--float`. The best checkpoint was fetched locally to `experiments/qdr156/quadrotor/1779265040848/0000000024903680.bin`. | Sensor-visible exploration is now learnable from zero on random blocks, but the first learned policy is a narrow coverage-gate optimizer. It solves open room and random blocks but crashes badly on fixed-layout/corridor/maze-style occlusions. Next limit: build a harder explore-v0 gauntlet and train/evaluate across mixed occluded layouts, not just random blocks. Also add native-bin visual support or a float deterministic eval path before promoting visual behavior. |
| QDR-155 | `indoor_explore_sensor_visible_coverage_v0` | Does changing exploration credit from visited-position cells to onboard-ray-visible cells make the task match real exploration better without adding privileged actor inputs? Hypothesis: a stationary or slow-moving drone should reveal multiple cells through its lidar/ray fan, coverage should increase from sensor visibility rather than physical cell traversal, and local smoke eval should still build and report coverage/new-cell metrics. | Passed as an environment-shape smoke, not a learning result. Code change: `qr_mark_visible_explored` ray-marches each deployable range ray and rewards newly observed valid free cells with `QR_EXPLORE_VISIBLE_CELL_REWARD=0.08`; default exploration success threshold is now `0.85`, and exploration timeout penalty is `0` so timeout is a fixed measurement budget rather than a harsh training failure. `scripts/quadrotor4.sh build-cpu` passed. Stationary zero-action, 64-step smoke at center spawn: open room coverage `52.48%`, random blocks `42.31%`, layout mixed `68.18%`; all below the new success gate. Random-block 128-step action smoke: zero action stayed at `42.31%` coverage and timed out, constant forward action reached `85.39%` coverage without crashing, and slow forward-turn reached `85.34%` without crashing. | The old `10%`/`2.75%` exploration thresholds were invalid once coverage means sensor-visible cells; a 360-degree lidar can map much of a small open room from a single pose. The new v0 benchmark should focus on occluded/random-block layouts, coverage-over-horizon, crash rate, and revisit/stagnation metrics before adding hidden target search. |
| QDR-154 | `swiftlite_resettable_rollout_search_teacher` | Can resettable hard-state replay plus privileged simulator rollouts produce a stronger recovery expert than one-step waypoint/ray heuristics? Hypothesis: if the next missing piece is expert quality, a short-horizon MPC-style search over deployable velocity commands from each exact hard state should rescue more QDR-145 failures than QDR-152/QDR-153, without changing actor observations or simulator dynamics. | Failed as a useful expert, but the resettable search machinery works. Added `--teacher-mode rollout_search`: it snapshots the current hard state, scores short constant-action rollouts in the simulator, commits the best action briefly, then hands back to the waypoint teacher. The first online-replan version was too slow and was killed; bounded one-shot search is the viable diagnostic. Fair same-setup waypoint baseline on `64` hard states, `rewind=64`, horizon `512`: actor failed `63/64`, waypoint teacher rescued `32/64` (`50.00%`). Rollout search with `--search-horizon 24 --search-commit-steps 24` rescued `27/64`; `--search-horizon 16 --search-commit-steps 4` rescued `32/64`; best tested `--search-horizon 24 --search-commit-steps 8` rescued `33/64` (`51.56%`). A richer candidate set (`--search-horizon 32 --search-commit-steps 8 --search-directions 32 --search-speeds 0.2 0.4 0.7 1.0`) fell back to `32/64`. | Do not turn this local constant-action MPC teacher into labels. It only adds `+1/64` over waypoint in the best case and often hurts. The useful conclusion is sharper: resettable hard-state replay is available, but the stronger privilege must be a real planner/trajectory optimizer or a resettable RL objective, not a shallow local action search. |
| QDR-153 | `swiftlite_ray_avoid_teacher_hardstate_ceiling` | Does a stronger resettable hard-state expert rescue QDR-145 pre-crash states better than the simple privileged waypoint teacher? Hypothesis: waypoint labels fail because they ignore local collision geometry when the actor is off the nominal path; adding deployable ray-based repulsion and clearance speed scaling to the privileged waypoint direction should rescue substantially more fixed hard states without changing actor observations or simulator dynamics. | Failed. Added `--teacher-mode ray_avoid` to `scripts/quadrotor4_hardstate_teacher_probe.py`; it combines privileged waypoint direction with deployable 24-ray repulsion and clearance speed scaling. Command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh hardstate-teacher-probe experiments/qdr145/asym_mixed15_from_qdr144_s88/qdr135_asym_ppo_000005.bin --seed 88 --route-goal-count 3 --total-agents 64 --num-buffers 1 --num-threads 1 --hard-states 64 --rewind 64 --replay-horizon 512 --max-collect-steps 8192 --teacher-mode ray_avoid`. Same hard-state geometry as QDR-152: mean min ray `0.555m`, p10 `0.246m`, p50 `0.521m`. Actor replay failed `63/64`. Ray-avoid teacher failed `34/64` and rescued `29/64` (`45.31%`), worse than the simple waypoint teacher's `32/64` (`50.00%`) on the same setup. Slower/stronger variants (`--teacher-max-speed 0.5 --teacher-speed-gain 1.5 --avoid-radius 1.2 --avoid-gain 1.5`; and `--teacher-max-speed 0.35 --teacher-speed-gain 1.0 --avoid-radius 1.2 --avoid-gain 2.0`) both rescued only `28/64` (`43.75%`). | Do not promote local ray-repulsion as the recovery expert. A naive potential-field teacher either hesitates or still steers into unrecoverable local geometry. The next expert needs real privileged planning/trajectory optimization or resettable RL from hard states, not another one-step steering heuristic. |
| QDR-152 | `swiftlite_resettable_teacher_hardstate_ceiling` | On exact QDR-145 pre-crash states from QDR-151, can the privileged waypoint teacher recover where the deployable actor fails? Hypothesis: if hard-state replay is the right next branch, then replaying the QDR-145 actor from captured states should reproduce failures, while stepping the same physical states with privileged waypoint teacher actions should avoid many of those failures. | Failed as a training-label ceiling; passed as diagnosis. Added `scripts/quadrotor4_hardstate_teacher_probe.py`, which collects paired actor/privileged snapshots and replays actor vs teacher on identical physical states. `rewind=8`, `128` hard states: actor failed `128/128`; waypoint teacher failed `114/128`, rescued only `14/128` (`10.94%`). `rewind=16`: teacher rescued `49/128` (`38.28%`). `rewind=32`: teacher rescued `55/128` (`42.97%`). `rewind=64`, `128` states, horizon `256`: teacher rescued `57/128` (`44.53%`). Longer horizon check with `64` states, `rewind=64`, horizon `512`: actor failed `63/64`; teacher failed `31/64`, rescued `32/64` (`50.00%`). Those `64` states were not all last-moment collisions: mean min ray was `0.555m`, p10 `0.246m`, p50 `0.521m`, mean target-distance observation `0.379`. | Exact replay shows the current simple privileged waypoint teacher is not a strong recovery expert. This explains why repeated one-shot recovery/DAgger saturates: many off-distribution states are not rescued by the labels we train toward. The next engineering build should create a stronger privileged expert or a resettable hard-state RL objective before more supervised correction. |
| QDR-151 | `swiftlite_resettable_hard_state_replay_probe` | Can we snapshot and restore an exact native quadrotor hard state, including the MinGRU hidden state held by the evaluator, so a QDR-145 obstacle-contact failure can be replayed deterministically? Hypothesis: if resettable hard-state replay is a viable next method family, restoring one pre-failure env state plus actor recurrent state should reproduce the same action/observation/reward/terminal trajectory within numerical tolerance. | Passed. Added `VecEnv.snapshot(env_id)` and `VecEnv.restore(env_id, snapshot)` to the native bindings, with restore preserving vector buffer pointers and recomputing observations. Command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh replay-probe experiments/qdr145/asym_mixed15_from_qdr144_s88/qdr135_asym_ppo_000005.bin --seed 88 --route-goal-count 3 --total-agents 64 --num-buffers 1 --num-threads 1 --rewind 8 --replay-horizon 64 --max-collect-steps 4096`. It captured env `30` at step `102`, snapshot size `5472` bytes. Two restores had `restore_obs_delta=0`; replay comparison had `len_a=9`, `len_b=9`, `action_delta=0`, `obs_delta=0`, `reward_delta=0`, `terminal_delta=0`, `replay_exact=1`. | Resettable hard-state replay is feasible. The next branch should use fixed pre-crash snapshots as a regression set and compare baseline actor, privileged waypoint teacher, and candidate recovery policies on identical hard states. Do not keep relying only on sampled failure windows when exact replay is now available. |
| QDR-150 | `swiftlite_asym_rl_from_raw24_distill_goals3` | Can asymmetric privileged-critic PPO rescue the best raw-ray sequential distillation checkpoint, or is the current direct actor too far below the useful basin? Hypothesis: starting from QDR-147, a short privileged-critic PPO phase should improve three-goal direct routing if the remaining gap is reward/advantage shaping rather than the actor observation/training distribution itself. | Failed. GPU command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh asym-ppo --load-actor experiments/qdr147/qdr147_raw24_nativehist_goals3_s88.bin --load-action-std 0.05 --output experiments/qdr150/asym_from_qdr147_raw24_goals3_s88 --network MinGRU --critic-network MinGRU --seed 88 --total-agents 4096 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --max-steps 1024 --num-rays 24 --route-goal-count 3 --updates 40 --horizon 128 --ppo-epochs 2 --actor-lr 0.00003 --critic-lr 0.0003 --clip-coef 0.05 --kl-coef 0.02 --ent-coef 0.0 --action-std 0.05 --save-interval 5 --print-interval 5 --eval-after --episodes 4096 --max-eval-steps 50000 --log-interval 64`. Deterministic update-5 eval: `30.52%` success, `37.44%` obstacle, `32.05%` timeout. Final eval: `14.14%` success and `85.86%` obstacle. | Asymmetric PPO only works as a small refinement when the actor is already in a competent basin, as in QDR-145. Starting from the weak QDR-147 sequential student, PPO rapidly drifts into obstacle contacts. Do not retry this branch without either a stronger actor initialization, resettable hard-state replay, or a safer actor update/early-stop mechanism. |
| QDR-149 | `swiftlite_full_dagger_raw24_goals3` | Does stronger DAgger-style induced-state correction fix the QDR-147 sequential cloning failure? Hypothesis: if QDR-147 failed mainly because the student was trained on too much teacher-controlled state distribution (`max_student_rollout_prob=0.5`), then ramping to full student rollouts (`1.0`) with privileged labels should improve pure-student three-goal eval beyond QDR-147's `40.58%`. | Failed. GPU command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh distill-train --output experiments/qdr149/qdr149_raw24_nativehist_full_dagger_goals3_s88.pt --network MinGRU --seed 88 --total-agents 4096 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --max-steps 1024 --num-rays 24 --route-goal-count 3 --route-perception-obs 0 --native-action-history-obs --updates 24000 --learning-rate 0.0003 --teacher-rollout-updates 1000 --max-student-rollout-prob 1.0 --action-std 0.05 --print-interval 500 --eval-after --episodes 4096 --max-eval-steps 50000 --log-interval 64`. Mixed-rollout success collapsed as student probability rose: about `95%` near `0.4` student probability, `80%` near `0.78`, and `61.93%` at full student rollout. Pure-student eval: `37.21%` three-goal, `62.79%` obstacle contacts, no timeouts; one-goal deterministic sanity eval after conversion was `71.85%`. | Full student-rollout one-step correction made QDR-147 worse on the main three-goal gate. Do not keep increasing the student rollout fraction in this labeler; the teacher action target is not enough to stabilize induced sequential states. |
| QDR-148 | `swiftlite_native_ppo_raw24_direct_goals3_from_zero` | Can corrected PufferLib 4 PPO learn the raw 24-ray direct three-goal task from zero without the privileged teacher, now that trainer parity issues are fixed? Hypothesis: if the direct navigation setup is fundamentally easy enough for the current observation/reward/interface, PPO from zero should at least match or beat QDR-147's `40.58%` three-goal student and ideally approach the QDR-139/QDR-145 plateau. If it fails, the blocker is not just bad teacher cloning; the direct reward/observation/control formulation is still too hard for from-zero PPO. | Failed. GPU command: `QUAD_TIMESTEPS=50000000 scripts/quadrotor4.sh train-gpu --checkpoint-dir experiments/qdr148 --log-dir logs/qdr148 --checkpoint-interval 20 --eval-episodes 4096 --env.seed 88 --train.seed 88 --torch.network MinGRU --policy.use-bias 1 --env.route-direct-obs 1 --env.route-wall-obs 1 --env.route-perception-obs 0 --env.num-rays 24 --env.action-history-obs 1 --env.route-target-obs-mode 1 --env.route-goal-count 3 --env.max-steps 1024`. Throughput was about `2.3-2.5M SPS`. Native stochastic checkpoint eval over 4096 episodes found best checkpoint `experiments/qdr148/quadrotor/1779224188103/0000000026476544.bin` at `30.94%` success with `69.06%` obstacle contacts and no timeouts. Later checkpoints stayed around `19-23%` with about `77-81%` obstacle contacts. Deterministic PyTorch eval is currently blocked because native PufferLib checkpoints are flat fp32 files, not Torch state dicts. | From-zero PPO does not solve this direct three-goal formulation. It learns progress but mostly turns into obstacle-contact behavior, and it underperforms the weak QDR-147 cloned student. This strengthens the blocker: direct reward plus current deployable obs/control is not easy enough for vanilla PPO, even after trainer parity fixes. Add a native-weight deterministic converter before promoting native PPO results. |
| QDR-147 | `swiftlite_nativehist_rawray_distill_goals3` | Is QDR-146's collapse caused by the 12-ray affordance observation, or by the sequential distillation setup itself? Hypothesis: with the same native-action-history distillation code but the original 24 raw rays and no affordance (`26 + 24 + 6 + 4 = 60`), pure-student eval should recover substantially more of the teacher than QDR-146. If it does not, the blocker is induced-distribution/BC on sequential routes, not perception feature choice. | Failed as a solution, passed as isolation. GPU command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh distill-train --output experiments/qdr147/qdr147_raw24_nativehist_goals3_s88.pt --network MinGRU --seed 88 --total-agents 4096 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --max-steps 1024 --num-rays 24 --route-goal-count 3 --route-perception-obs 0 --native-action-history-obs --updates 16000 --learning-rate 0.0003 --teacher-rollout-updates 1000 --max-student-rollout-prob 0.5 --action-std 0.05 --print-interval 500 --eval-after --episodes 4096 --max-eval-steps 50000 --log-interval 64`. Converted checkpoint: `experiments/qdr147/qdr147_raw24_nativehist_goals3_s88.bin`. Deterministic native eval: `67.44%` one-goal, `40.58%` three-goal, all failures obstacle contacts. This is much better than QDR-146's `26.63%` three-goal but still far below QDR-139/QDR-145. | The affordance/ray-budget change hurt, but raw-ray one-step privileged distillation is also not enough. The gap is induced-distribution/control behavior, not lack of a teacher ceiling. Next test should verify whether from-zero native PPO can learn this direct three-goal formulation at all before adding more hand-built perception. |
| QDR-146 | `swiftlite_fixed_obs_affordance_budget` | Does the QDR-143 deployable-perception blocker disappear if we keep the native 60-float actor observation contract fixed by trading ray resolution for local affordance features? Hypothesis: `num_rays=12`, `route_wall_obs=1`, `route_perception_obs=3`, and native `action_history_obs=1` gives the actor the same total obs size as the QDR-139/QDR-145 contract (`26 + 12 + 6 + 12 + 4 = 60`) while exposing target-aligned clearance/gap cues from onboard rays only. If perception is the current bottleneck, a from-zero privileged-training run should improve sequential direct routing without adding privileged actor inputs. | Failed. Added `--num-rays/--ray-length` to native eval scripts and `--native-action-history-obs` to `scripts/quadrotor4_privileged_distill.py`. CPU smoke proved the native 60-float contract and conversion path. GPU command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh distill-train --output experiments/qdr146/qdr146_affordance12_nativehist_goals3_s88.pt --network MinGRU --seed 88 --total-agents 4096 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --max-steps 1024 --num-rays 12 --route-goal-count 3 --route-perception-obs 3 --native-action-history-obs --updates 16000 --learning-rate 0.0003 --teacher-rollout-updates 1000 --max-student-rollout-prob 0.5 --action-std 0.05 --print-interval 500 --eval-after --episodes 4096 --max-eval-steps 50000 --log-interval 64`. Converted checkpoint: `experiments/qdr146/qdr146_affordance12_nativehist_goals3_s88.bin`. Teacher ceiling on the same 3-goal setup was `95.97%` success with `4.03%` obstacle. Pure-student native eval was `54.87%` one-goal and `26.63%` three-goal, all failures obstacle contacts. | The fixed-size affordance observation is valid infrastructure but a bad solution. It did not just fail to beat QDR-145; it failed the one-goal gate badly. The low imitation loss plus high teacher ceiling says this is an induced-distribution/perception-contract failure, not an env crash or teacher ceiling issue. Next isolation: raw 24-ray native-action-history distillation before touching new perception features again. |
| QDR-143 | `swiftlite_affordance_distill_goals3` | Does a deployable local affordance observation derived from onboard rays make sequential direct routing easier when trained from a privileged waypoint teacher, instead of repeatedly correcting the same raw-ray actor? | Blocked before GPU. Added plumbing so `scripts/quadrotor4_privileged_distill.py` accepts `--route-perception-obs` and `scripts/quadrotor4_eval_headless.py` can eval matching layouts. CPU smoke command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh distill-train --cpu --output /private/tmp/qdr143_smoke.pt --network MinGRU --seed 88 --total-agents 64 --num-buffers 1 --num-threads 1 --max-steps 128 --route-goal-count 3 --route-perception-obs 3 --updates 4 --learning-rate 0.0003 --teacher-rollout-updates 1 --max-student-rollout-prob 0.2 --action-std 0.05 --print-interval 1 --eval-after --episodes 32 --max-eval-steps 512`. This counts as an infrastructure blocker, not a policy result. |
| QDR-144 | `swiftlite_mixed_length_recovery_curriculum` | Does collecting QDR-139 failures under a mixed route-length curriculum (`1..5` goals) produce a more robust sequential direct actor than fixed three-goal correction? | Marginal pass, current best unless superseded. Held-out deterministic means on seeds `88/90/91`: `direct_one_goal=94.87%`, `direct_goals3=65.44%` with `34.56%` obstacle / `0.00%` timeout, `direct_goals5=50.75%`, `direct_combo=54.70%`. Trained `experiments/qdr142/qdr142_iter_dagger_lr3e6_s88.bin` from QDR-139. Exact command: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh failure-recovery --load-actor experiments/qdr139/qdr139_goals3_recovery_lr1e5_s88.bin --load-action-std 0.05 --output experiments/qdr142/qdr142_iter_dagger_lr3e6_s88.bin --network MinGRU --seed 88 --total-agents 4096 --num-buffers 2 --num-threads 16 --max-steps 1024 --route-goal-count 3 --window 24 --max-failure-windows 4096 --max-anchor-windows 8192 --max-collect-steps 30000 --epochs 1 --batch-size 256 --learning-rate 0.000003 --recovery-weight 1.0 --failure-anchor-weight 0.5 --general-anchor-weight 2.0 --eval-after --episodes 4096 --max-eval-steps 50000 --log-interval 64`. Deterministic eval means on seeds `88/90/91`: `direct_one_goal=94.71%`, `direct_goals3=64.91%` with `35.09%` obstacle / `0.00%` timeout, `direct_goals5=50.21%`, `direct_combo=54.75%`. | Do not treat a second tiny recovery pulse as the fix. It preserves one-goal and helps five-goal versus QDR-139, but misses the required QDR-139 three-goal gate (`65.05%`) and slightly worsens obstacle contact. The sequential hard-state distribution is near saturation for one-shot cloning; further progress likely needs resettable replay, a changed deployable observation, or a different actor objective rather than repeated correction pulses. |
| QDR-140 | `swiftlite_goal_baseline_reconfirm` | Reconfirm the current QDR-136/QDR-139 baseline required by the broader research-SOTA goal across `direct_one_goal`, `direct_goals3`, `direct_goals5`, and `direct_combo` on seeds `88/90/91`. | Passed. Deterministic eval with `scripts/quadrotor4.sh eval-headless` reconfirmed the current reference table. QDR-136 means: `direct_one_goal=94.91%`, `direct_goals3=48.41%`, `direct_goals5=36.04%`, `direct_combo=58.82%`; `direct_goals3` failure mix is `24.38%` obstacle / `27.20%` timeout. QDR-139 means: `direct_one_goal=94.68%`, `direct_goals3=65.05%`, `direct_goals5=45.88%`, `direct_combo=55.40%`; `direct_goals3` failure mix is `34.94%` obstacle / `0%` timeout. Command shape: `scripts/quadrotor4.sh eval-headless <checkpoint> --network MinGRU --seed <seed> --route-direct-obs 1 --route-wall-obs 1 --route-target-obs-mode 1 --action-history-obs 1 --stress-profile <profile> --episodes 4096 --total-agents 4096 --horizon 64 --max-rollouts 512 --slowly --deterministic`. | Baseline requirement for the broader goal is satisfied. QDR-139 is the sequential direct-routing checkpoint to beat, but the promotion gate must require lower obstacle contact, not just higher success. QDR-136 remains the safer one-goal direct checkpoint. |
| QDR-139 | `swiftlite_goals3_failure_recovery` | Does the QDR-136 near-failure recovery pulse transfer to the new sequential direct-routing limit if we collect hard states on `direct_goals3` instead of one-goal episodes? | Passed as a useful but incomplete improvement. Starting from QDR-136, collect deployable/privileged failure windows with `route_goal_count=3` and `max_steps=1024`, then apply one recovery epoch. Full `3e-5` recovery was too strong: `direct_goals3` rose to `56.13%` mean but one-goal dropped to about `90.68%` and all failures became obstacle contacts. Smaller `1e-5` recovery is the useful checkpoint: `experiments/qdr139/qdr139_goals3_recovery_lr1e5_s88.bin`. It preserved one-goal success at `94.56%`/`94.78%`/`94.70%`, improved `direct_goals3` to `65.02%`/`65.68%`/`64.45%`, and improved `direct_goals5` to `37.04%`/`50.60%`/`50.00%` on seeds `88`/`90`/`91`. | Sequential hard-state data matters. QDR-139 gives a real `+16.6` point mean gain on `direct_goals3` without sacrificing the one-goal gate, but it mostly converts timeout/hesitation into obstacle contacts. The next limit is safer sequential recovery: keep the extra progress while restoring clearance, likely with resettable hard-state replay around post-goal transitions plus an explicit clearance/speed anchor. |
| QDR-138 | `swiftlite_direct_actor_stress_gate` | What breaks next after QDR-136/QDR-137 improve the one-goal deployable direct actor to about `95%`? | Passed as diagnosis. Extended `scripts/quadrotor4_eval_headless.py` with named direct-actor stress profiles: `direct_one_goal`, `direct_goals3`, `direct_goals5`, `direct_z_band`, `direct_dynamics_light`, and `direct_combo`. QDR-136 remains best on the easy gate: `direct_one_goal=94.90%`; z-band and light dynamics only drop to `92.99%` and `93.34%` on seed 88. Sequential routing collapses: QDR-136 `direct_goals3` is `48.41%` mean over seeds `88/90/91`, `direct_goals5` is `36.04%`, and `direct_combo` is `58.82%`. QDR-137 is not more robust: `direct_goals3=44.83%`, `direct_goals5=35.77%`, `direct_combo=41.11%`. | The next harder limit is not target height or light dynamics. It is multi-goal direct navigation under partial observability and compounding local decisions. QDR-136 is the better base checkpoint; QDR-137 trades obstacle hits for timeouts on longer routes. Next training should target sequential/direct routing explicitly, preferably with deterministic hard-state collection or resettable replay around post-goal transitions and late-route hidden-target states. |
| QDR-137 | `swiftlite_asym_ppo_recovery_replay` | Can the QDR-136 hard-state correction be integrated into asymmetric PPO as a small online replay component instead of a separate one-shot cloning stage? | Close but not promoted. Extended `scripts/quadrotor4_asym_ppo.py` with a near-failure replay buffer collected from synchronized actor/critic rollouts, privileged recovery labels, optional separate recovery LR, and anchor loss. Direct PPO plus replay (`experiments/qdr137/asym_recovery_replay_s88`) improved over QDR-135 but underperformed QDR-136: best seed-88 checkpoint update 3 was `94.42%`. Replay-only with separate LR (`experiments/qdr137/replay_only_s88`) reached `94.16%`. Larger-buffer replay-only (`experiments/qdr137/replay_late_s88/qdr135_asym_ppo_000009.bin`) was best: `94.79%`/`94.76%`/`94.67%` on seeds `88`/`90`/`91`. QDR-136 remains slightly better on mean: `94.90%`/`95.21%`/`94.61%`. | Useful negative result. Online sampled failure replay reproduces most of QDR-136's gain over QDR-135, but does not beat deterministic one-shot hard-state collection. The next limit is recovery data quality and reset/replay mechanics, not loss weighting: sampled PPO rollouts collect noisier, fewer useful pre-crash windows unless we add deterministic hard-state collection, resettable hard states, or a real DAgger-style recovery loop. |
| QDR-136 | `swiftlite_failure_recovery_bc` | Can we reduce the remaining obstacle contacts by collecting only near-crash state windows and training a recovery update from privileged waypoint labels, instead of training on mostly solved episodes? | Passed. Added `scripts/quadrotor4_failure_recovery.py`: run the QDR-135 direct actor, collect deployable/privileged observation windows before negative terminal rewards, train against privileged waypoint recovery actions with an anchor to the original actor, then deterministic-evaluate on seeds `88/90/91`. Best checkpoint `experiments/qdr136/qdr136_failure_recovery_tail24_ep1_s88.bin` improved success to `94.90%`/`95.21%`/`94.61%`, all remaining failures obstacle hits. Same-command QDR-135 baseline was `91.94%`/`92.24%`/`92.56%`. Brackets: one epoch was best; two epochs dropped to `94.50%`/`95.14%`/`94.29%`; four epochs washed out (`91.87%`/`92.66%`/`92.12%`). Tail-only variants were useful but slightly weaker: tail `3` one epoch `94.60%`/`94.15%`/`94.05%`, tail `6` one epoch `94.65%`/`94.29%`/`94.04%`, tail `12` one epoch `94.78%`/`94.50%`/`94.14%`. | Meaningful progress. A small near-failure supervised pulse reduces obstacle contacts by about `2.7` points over QDR-135, but repeated cloning drifts and erases the gain. The next limit is not generic PPO tuning; it is how to integrate hard-state recovery into the online/asymmetric trainer without overfitting or overwriting the deployable actor. |
| QDR-135 | `swiftlite_asymmetric_critic_ppo` | Can PPO fine-tuning improve QDR-130 when the actor sees only deployable direct-target observations but the critic sees privileged waypoint/planner observations? | Passed as first proof. Added `scripts/quadrotor4_asym_ppo.py`: two synchronized native envs receive the same sampled actions; actor obs uses `route_direct_obs=1`, critic obs uses `route_direct_obs=0`, both include native previous-action observation. Actor initializes from the converted QDR-130 student and is regularized by KL to that actor. Best checkpoint `experiments/qdr135/asym_ppo_kl005_s88/qdr135_asym_ppo_000020.bin` evaluated at `91.94%`/`92.24%`/`92.56%` on seeds `88`/`90`/`91`, all remaining failures obstacle hits. Baseline converted QDR-130 was `91.01%` on seed 88 and original QDR-130 was `90.96%`/`91.19%`/`91.55%`. Longer training drifted (`91.64%` at update 40, then `90.11%`/`89.40%`), and strong KL `1.0` underperformed (`91.38%` best). | This is the first post-QDR-130 method that moves the deployable direct actor in the right direction. The gain is modest, about `+0.9` to `+1.0` points, but held-out seeds agree. The next limit is controlling actor drift and using the privileged critic more effectively: early stopping around update `20`, stronger value training, better advantage quality, or a true asymmetric trainer with minibatches/state resets. |
| QDR-134 | `swiftlite_distill_then_rl_finetune_prev_action` | Can the research-style next phase, RL fine-tuning after privileged distillation, improve the QDR-130 deployable student? | Mixed. Added native `action_history_obs=1` to the C env, raising the PufferLib native observation size to `60`, and added deterministic PyTorch-backend eval for converted checkpoints. Converted QDR-130 with previous action preserved evaluated at `91.01%`, matching the original QDR-130 bridge. PPO fine-tuning from std `0.20` did not improve: best `90.78%`, final `87.37%`. Lowering initial std to `0.05` improved sampled rollout success from about `43%` to `68-79%`, but deterministic fine-tune still did not beat base: best `90.95%`, final `90.52%`. Std `0.02` sampled worse (`64.97%`) due timeouts. | Passed the infrastructure bridge, failed the naive fine-tune. The next limit is not previous-action plumbing anymore; it is the RL objective/update. PPO is not extracting useful improvement from the distilled student under this setup. Next test should use a safer fine-tune method: freeze or heavily regularize the actor, train value first, lower policy LR/clip, or implement asymmetric/privileged critic rather than plain PPO on noisy direct-observation rollouts. |
| QDR-133 | `swiftlite_privileged_online_bptt_short_chunks` | Does QDR-132 fail because `64`-step chunks give too few optimizer updates and stale recurrent states? | Failed. Same total exposure as QDR-132, but `seq_len=16`, `seq_updates=2000`, and capped scheduled sampling. Mixed-rollout training stayed near-perfect for most of the run, but pure-student eval was only `52.32%` success / `47.68%` crashes. | More optimizer steps did not fix the cloning gap. The deployable actor fails under its own induced distribution, matching the classic DAgger/compounding-error failure mode. Move to RL fine-tuning/asymmetric actor-critic instead of more imitation-only variants. |
| QDR-132 | `swiftlite_privileged_online_bptt_mingru` | Was QDR-131 failing because reset-aligned chunks only trained the first `64` episode steps? | Failed as a policy improvement, passed as a diagnosis. Full student rollout collapsed to `56.42%` success. Capping scheduled sampling at `0.6` kept mixed-rollout chunk success near `99%`, but pure eval was still only `49.69%`. | Reset-aligned chunks were not the only issue. Large-chunk sequence imitation is a poor fit here, likely because it has very few optimizer steps and trains on stale recurrent states. The one-step detached QDR-130 path remains much stronger. |
| QDR-131 | `swiftlite_privileged_sequence_distill_mingru` | Does true sequence/BPTT distillation reduce QDR-130's remaining `8-9%` obstacle contacts versus one-step detached MinGRU imitation? | Failed. Reset-aligned sequence chunks with full student rollout wrote `experiments/qdr131/qdr131_sequence_mingru_s88.pt` and evaluated at `54.20%` success / `45.80%` crashes. Capping scheduled sampling at `0.5` wrote `qdr131_sequence_cap05_mingru_s88.pt` and evaluated at `48.44%` success / `51.56%` crashes. | The sequence idea was not invalidated, but the implementation was. Resetting every update trains mostly clean episode prefixes, while full eval needs later mid-episode recovery. Do not rerun reset-aligned chunks except as a regression check. |
| QDR-130 | `swiftlite_privileged_distill_direct_actor_v0` | Can a planner-free direct-target actor learn the one-goal mixed-layout task from privileged waypoint-teacher distillation, beating the direct-from-zero cliff without exposing waypoint/path breadcrumbs at deployment? | Passed first proof. `scripts/quadrotor4_privileged_distill.py` runs two synchronized envs: privileged teacher sees waypoint obs, student sees direct final-target obs plus previous action. Teacher eval: `99.98%`. Feedforward student failed at `57.86%`. MinGRU student `experiments/qdr130/qdr130_privileged_student_mingru_s88.pt` passed deterministic eval on seeds `88/90/91`: `90.96%`/`91.19%`/`91.55%`, all remaining failures obstacle contacts. | Privileged distillation plus memory beats the direct-from-zero cliff (`40.65%` QDR-115, `83.65%` QDR-118, `86.53%` QDR-120 continuation) without deploying waypoints. The direct actor is partially observable; feedforward imitation is not enough. Next limit is the remaining `8-9%` obstacle contacts, likely requiring true sequence training/asymmetric critic and then thrust/body-rate actions. |
| QDR-129 | `pufferlib4_velocity_bias_fanin_init_s88` | Does PyTorch-style nonzero fan-in bias initialization rescue weak seed `88` without changing the env or reward? | Failed. Late 4096-episode sweep best same checkpoint: `88.49%` success, `11.51%` obstacle hits. Reverted the init change; do not retest this as the next parity fix. |

Stronger clearance improves the solved one-goal rung and is competitive on fixed 5-goal, but it still leaves the same obstacle-contact limit. |

Stronger clearance moved the hard frontier on seed 91; remaining failures are still obstacle contacts under multi-goal/combo stress. |

Limits mean `97.84%`, weakest `combo_hard=94.63%`, `goals_5=94.92%`. | Useful but not promoted. Lower progress pressure improves QDR-104 hard training, but still does not beat QDR-102 zero-shot clearance on the hard frontier. |

QDR-106 stress mean `94.82%`, weakest `goals_10=86.13%`, `combo_long=92.29%`. | Passed as diagnosis. ## Useful Commands

QDR-138 direct-actor stress eval:

```bash
scripts/quadrotor4.sh eval-headless experiments/qdr136/qdr136_failure_recovery_tail24_ep1_s88.bin \
  --network MinGRU --seed 88 --route-direct-obs 1 --route-wall-obs 1 \
  --route-target-obs-mode 1 --action-history-obs 1 \
  --stress-profile direct_goals3 --episodes 4096 --total-agents 4096 \
  --horizon 64 --max-rollouts 512 --slowly --deterministic
```

Change `--stress-profile` to `direct_one_goal`, `direct_goals5`,
`direct_z_band`, `direct_dynamics_light`, or `direct_combo`.

Current strongest Swift-lite checkpoint:

```bash
scripts/quadrotor4.sh eval-headless \
  experiments/qdr145/asym_mixed15_from_qdr144_s88/qdr135_asym_ppo_000005.bin \
  --network MinGRU --seed 88 --route-direct-obs 1 --route-wall-obs 1 \
  --route-target-obs-mode 1 --action-history-obs 1 \
  --stress-profile direct_goals3 --episodes 4096 --total-agents 4096 \
  --horizon 64 --max-rollouts 512 --slowly --deterministic
```

Visual smoke/inspection command:

```bash
scripts/quadrotor4.sh visual-eval \
  experiments/qdr145/asym_mixed15_from_qdr144_s88/qdr135_asym_ppo_000005.bin \
  --stress-profile direct_goals3 --episodes 3 --seed 88
```

## Current Lessons

- The custom env was not fundamentally broken. The biggest blockers were invalid
  maze geometry, overly conservative waypoint margin, and script defaults
  overriding scenario/profile defaults.
- The PufferLib 4.0 port exposed three trainer/config mismatches before any new
  environment idea was needed: native reward clipping distorted calibrated
  rewards, native training ignored the configured policy network, and decoder
  init was too large for continuous actor outputs. Fixing these moved the 4.0
  velocity benchmark from roughly `55-66%` to `90.79%`.
- QDR-127 exposed a fourth major 4.0 parity mismatch: native linear layers had
  no biases while the PyTorch reference encoder/MLP/decoder did. Optional
  biases raise the 4.0 velocity benchmark to `97.91%`, leaving a much smaller
  obstacle-contact gap to the old `99.46%` recipe.
- QDR-128 shows that the biased 4.0 MLP still does not train robustly from zero
  across seeds. Weak seeds peak around `93-94%` and often drift lower late in
  training. All failures remain obstacle contacts, so the feedback loop should
  target model/trainer parity and stability before adding harder env features.
- QDR-129 ruled out PyTorch-style fan-in bias initialization as the missing
  parity piece. It worsened seed `88`, so the promoted 4.0 code keeps zero bias
  initialization for now.
- QDR-130 is the first positive result on the new Swift-style track. A
  direct-target actor with no waypoint breadcrumbs crosses `90%` only when
  trained from a privileged waypoint teacher and given recurrent memory. This
  validates the pivot away from optimizing the waypoint policy itself.
- QDR-136 is the first clear improvement on the direct deployable actor after
  QDR-135. One epoch of near-failure privileged recovery cloning improves the
  three-seed direct benchmark from `91.94-92.56%` to `94.61-95.21%`, but more
  epochs drift. Treat hard-state recovery as a short corrective update or an
  online replay component, not as another long imitation phase.
- QDR-137 shows that naive online replay is not enough by itself. Sampled
  rollout replay gets close to QDR-136 but does not beat deterministic
  hard-state collection, so the next recovery work should improve hard-state
  data quality or reset mechanics rather than just changing replay weights.
- QDR-138 makes the next harder limit clear. The deployable direct actor is no
  longer mainly failing on one-goal height/dynamics perturbations; it fails on
  sequential routing. QDR-136 drops from `94.90%` one-goal success to about
  `48%` on three goals and `36%` on five goals, with obstacle contacts and
  timeouts compounding after partial progress.
- QDR-139 proves that sequential hard-state data helps. A smaller `1e-5`
  recovery pulse from `direct_goals3` failures keeps one-goal success near
  QDR-136 while raising three-goal success to about `65%`. It is not solved:
  the policy becomes more decisive but now fails mostly by obstacle contact.
- QDR-141 through QDR-145 tested four broader method families under the new
  research-SOTA goal. Clearance-speed anchoring made the actor cautious again
  and lost the sequential gain. A second DAgger-style correction saturated near
  QDR-139. Deployable affordance observations are blocked in the current 4.0
  build because observation size is fixed at `60`. Mixed-length hard-state
  recovery plus a short asymmetric-critic fine-tune produced the current
  strongest checkpoint, QDR-145 update 5, but the improvement is marginal:
  `direct_goals3=65.44%` and obstacle contacts still average `34.56%`.
- The drone can now learn simple obstacle navigation from zero with a small
  feedforward policy and no teacher.
- `vel_ctrl_ki_z=4.0` is a low-level controller setting, not a full autopilot.
  It is necessary for disturbance margin in the velocity-action interface.
- The next real limit is not doorway transfer anymore. It is longer sequential
  routing, especially `goals_5` and combined multi-goal/noise/dynamics stress.
  Richer layout randomization and reducing waypoint help come after this stays
  robust.
- Naive direct or checkpoint-finetuned 5-goal training does not fix the limit.
  The promoted one-goal checkpoint remains the best current policy.
- Uniform 1..5 route-length curriculum from zero also does not fix the limit.
  It learns easier mixed-length episodes but underperforms on fixed 5-goal eval.
- Compact target-aligned perception features are not enough by themselves. The
  planar rays are already body-frame, so extra hand-picked perception features
  are not the current missing piece.
- Exposing the previous two policy commands does not help the current no-delay
  fixed-layout benchmark from zero. It may still matter for explicit action
  delay and actuator latency, but it should not be treated as a general fix.
- A naive ray-specific convolutional encoder is much worse than the flat MLP.
  More model capacity is not the near-term fix unless the architecture is tied
  to a sharper hypothesis.
- Training with `max_vel=0.8` does not reproduce the eval-only speed-cap gain.
  The policy still contacts obstacles, so the next probe should change the
  reward incentives for clearance/speed rather than externally narrowing the
  action interface.
- `route_clearance_scale=0.70` is the first post-baseline change that improves
  the hard seed-91 frontier: `limits` mean rose to `98.81%`, with
  `combo_hard=96.48%` and `goals_5=97.17%`. The one-goal rung is now too easy
  to guide iteration.
- Direct hard-combo training with clearance `0.70` is not automatically better.
  QDR-104 learned the stress distribution but regressed `goals_5` and had much
  higher velocity in weak cases, so the next hard-training probe should target
  speed/reward economics inside the harder distribution.
- Doubling dense velocity penalty to `0.08` is too blunt: QDR-105 was dominated
  by QDR-104. The next reward probe should reduce progress pressure directly
  while keeping the original velocity cost.
- Reducing dense progress pressure to `20.0` is the right direction relative to
  QDR-104, but hard-distribution training still does not beat the safer QDR-102
  zero-shot clearance checkpoint. The easy one-goal metric is now only a smoke
  test; longer-horizon stress is the useful signal.
- The new `stress` gauntlet exposes the next limit cleanly: `combo_long` and
  `goals_10` are much weaker than the old one-goal checks, and QDR-102 still
  beats the hard-trained QDR-106 checkpoint overall.
- Eval-only speed caps are not the solution: they can improve pure long
  horizons, but they hurt `combo_long` by creating timeouts.
- Training lower progress in the easy one-goal recipe is also not the solution:
  QDR-110 becomes faster and worse on fixed 5-goal and stress.
- QDR-111 shows current champion failures are mostly late-route `combo_long`
  cases in maze/center-block layouts where the final target is often hidden and
  the policy is relying on the waypoint scaffold.
- QDR-112 validates that hypothesis on seed 91. Appending real-drone-plausible
  final-target context improved fixed 5-goal from QDR-102's `95.80%` to
  `96.39%` and stress mean from `96.48%` to `97.64%`, with `combo_long`
  improving from `89.95%` to `94.44%`.
- The QDR-112 implementation initially exposed a real wrapper bug: the C env
  emitted 8 extra final-target observations, but the Python wrapper did not
  allocate them. That invalid run was stopped and the wrapper/eval/gauntlet
  plumbing was fixed before the cited result.
- QDR-113 shows the final-target context also changes what training
  distributions work. The old 1..5 curriculum failed in QDR-095, but with
  final-target context it improves fixed 5-goal to `97.71%` and stress mean to
  `98.35%`. The residual weak case is narrower now: `combo_long` under
  latency/noise/dynamics.
- QDR-114 prevents over-promotion. Final-target plus 1..5 curriculum is strong
  on fixed 5-goal across three seeds, but seed 90 collapses on long-horizon
  stress (`goals_10=52.05%`, `combo_long=58.89%`). The next promoted benchmark
  needs stress robustness, not just fixed-route success.
- QDR-115 shows the waypoint scaffold is still doing a lot of work. Direct
  final-target observations train only to `40.65%`, mostly obstacle contacts.
- QDR-116 shows the lower-level rate-action interface is also a real cliff.
  Simply swapping away from the velocity controller reaches only `43.03%` on the
  one-goal Swift-lite rung.
- QDR-117 adds working real-drone-profile plumbing. `micro_indoor_v0` trains and
  evaluates with action delay, previous-action observation, noise, and simple
  dynamics randomization, but the first result is only `84.67%` under that
  profile.

## Next Useful Tests

1. Promote QDR-139 as the current sequential direct-routing probe, but not as a
   solved benchmark. Next improve safety: keep the `direct_goals3` progress gain
   while reducing obstacle contacts with resettable hard-state replay,
   clearance/speed anchoring, or post-goal transition-specific data.
2. Keep QDR-136 as the safest one-goal deployable direct checkpoint; use QDR-139
   only when testing sequential direct-routing ideas.
3. Test a planner-free local affordance or memory change on the QDR-120 direct
   geodesic rung. Extra budget alone only reached `86.53%`, and failures are
   still hidden-target cases.
4. Diagnose QDR-114 seed 90 long-horizon failures against seed 88 on identical
   `goals_10` and `combo_long` episode seeds.
5. Make reduced-waypoint navigation easier in controlled steps: direct target
   plus one or two local planner hints, not full waypoint removal at once.
6. Build a rate-action pretraining rung before indoor obstacles: hover, move to
   visible target, then add the layout-mixed obstacle sampler.
7. Use `micro_indoor_v0` as a hard profile gate after the clean benchmark is
   stress-robust; do not calibrate it against real hardware until we know which
   synthetic knob dominates the failures.
8. Finish the PufferLib 4.0 reproduction before inventing harder tasks: compare
   the biased 4.0 MLP checkpoint against the old 3.x promoted checkpoint, then
   test exact old PyTorch-style init or action/logprob differences one at a
   time.
